, making it financially possible for them to attend and thrive. {'kept': 'Application Advice\nSkip Sub Navigation\nOur best application advice
, just for you.\n\nOur team compiled the best practical pieces of advice to move you toward a successful
, and possibly even joyful
, application. We offer them based on our own experience
, and with total confidence that you will
, in the end
, blaze your own trail of steps.\n\nStart where you are.\n\nBefore you look at colleges
, look (generously!) at yourself.\n\nAsk yourself questions. Such as: What courses have I loved? Why did I love them? What motivates me as a student? Is it intellectual engagement? Creative expression? Solving concrete problems? Discovering strange and unexpected things? Challenging convention?\n\nKeep asking! What motivates me as a human being? What makes me want to get out of bed in the morning? What frustrates me? In what ways do I want to improve? Who’s someone I admire?\n\nStart asking other people! Talk to people who know you well: friends and family
, teachers and counselors
, coaches and mentors. Talk to representatives at college fairs. Talk to current students. Talk to us. Or be counter-cultural and read the Wellesley 100 (100 of the reasons why we might think you might love Wellesley).\n\nGet organized.\n\nA little planning and preparation goes a long way.\n\nUse tools that work for you. Make a spreadsheet! Command your calendar to send you reminders! Bookmark your favorite online resources! Adjust your spam filter to accept email from your selected colleges! And so on.\n\nFollow the deadlines. Look into required standardized testing and consider the optimal time to take those test(s). Take note of application and financial aid deadlines. Open mail from colleges right away and note schedules for visit programs
, information sessions
, etc.\n\nPlan a road trip. Try to visit some of the colleges you’re considering. Being on campus
, talking to students
, feeling the vibe (an actual college admissions term)—these are invaluable. Can’t make it to campus? We’ll do everything we can to give you an immersive online experience.\n\nLook closer.\n\nThere are more than 5
,000 colleges and universities in the United States. You probably shouldn’t apply to all of them.\n\nMake a list of essentials. For example: Do you want to be close to a city? Close to home? Do you want a huge university
, a small college
, or something in between? Are you looking for special programs or majors? (See below for a little counterintuitive advice.) Try to generate a list of 10 to 20 schools that meet your essential qualifications. Then be ready to revise it.\n\nQuestion what’s essential. For example (this is the advice we mentioned above): Your major is not your life. Most students change majors at some point in their college career. And a major isn’t an ironclad
, lifetime commitment. On a related note
, ponder the value of a liberal arts education—an opportunity to explore a range of fields
, make connections across disciplines
, and practice the skills that any income-earning
, change-making person needs.\n\nConsider the excellence of women’s colleges. We’re just the tiniest bit biased here. And yet
, it just so happens that one of the world’s most powerful social and professional networks is made up of our alumnae. Who happen to be women. Not really a coincidence
, to be honest.\n\nWhen you’re ready to think about cost
, be bold.\n\nYes
, be bold. Apply to colleges based not on your family income but on your achievements and your aspirations.\n\nLook at the numbers. You’ll want to get this information from every college on your list: What percentage of students receive financial aid? What forms of aid are provided? What’s the amount of the average annual aid package? What’s the average loan debt of the most recent entering class?\n\nGet a rough sense of the true cost. Most selective liberal arts colleges (which may have a higher sticker price) offer financial aid to support families with calculated financial need. The result is that many families pay less (often a great deal less) than the listed price of the college—less even than they would pay at a public or state institution. Every college has a Net Price Calculator on its website—a tool that gives you a rough estimate of what your family would expect to pay. We also have our own MyinTuition calculator
, a simple
, straightforward tool developed by one of our professors.\n\nPay attention to deadlines! The deadlines for the FAFSA and CSS Profile really matter. A late application can affect your eligibility for financial aid.\n\nWrite an essay you’ll love.\n\nWhich might be different than an essay you think a college will love.\n\nSet reasonable expectations. You don’t need to have had extraordinary experiences to write an extraordinary essay. You don’t need to have done incomparable things to be interesting. An essay about a small
, seemingly insignificant event can be more powerful than an essay about how you’ve saved the world or overcome tragedy. Also: You’re not writing a novel. You should be able to do the work in about 500 words.\n\nFollow a reasonable process. Write several drafts. Let a draft sit for a few days
, then tackle it again. Share drafts with people you trust. Ask them: Does this sound like me? Is it interesting from the start? You know you’re done when you’ve used only the words you need to use to say only what you wanted to say.\n\nGive yourself a pep talk. Such as: I love who I am
, and no college will change that. I love my voice
, and I’m using it now. This is actually fun! I’m having fun! Even if I’m awkwardly telling myself so! I can’t be anyone other than myself
, and that’s enough.\n\nInfo for\nClose'
, 'droped': ''
}

, investments
, savings
, and college savings plans among other assets. An amount of a family’s assets are protected from being considered when determining the ability to pay for college. Families with assets significantly higher than what is typical for their income may not qualify for grants meeting the cost of tuition
, but may qualify for need based aid.\n\nWill I still be charged tuition if I meet the criteria for the Wellesley Tuition Promise?\n\nYes. , located in the Schneider Center
, is a one-stop destination for both prospective and returning students. The SFS staff is here to assist students and their families in understanding the aid application process
, parent and student loans
, payment plans
, and more. Whether or not you receive financial aid
, we are here to help your family figure out how to pay for college. We are confident that a Wellesley education is one of the best investments your family can make.\n\nWellesley may be more affordable than you think. Nearly 60% of Wellesley students receive aid. In 2025-2026
, over $84 million was awarded to students. Beginning in Fall 2027
, Wellesley students whose families earn less than $200
,000 with typical assets attend the College tuition-free. The average annual grant is $70
,519—that’s money you don't have to pay back.\n\nExplore below the three ways to get a sense of what your financial aid offer might be at Wellesley.\nInstant Net Price Estimator\n\nThe Instant Net Price Estimator provides a quick estimate of your cost to attend Wellesley. Select your number of siblings in college and family income
, and you’ll get an initial look at what a Wellesley education may cost you. The Instant Net Price Estimator was created by a Wellesley economics professor!\n\nInstant Net Price Estimator\nLET’S GET MORE SPECIFIC\nDetailed Estimates\nMyinTuition
, Wellesley’s Quick College Cost Estimator\n\nMyinTuition asks only six simple financial questions. By providing family income
, home value
, and savings

{'kept': 'Pause Video\nWhere You Belong descriptive video transcript\nThe Wellesley Tuition Promise\nSkip Sub Navigation\n\nStarting in fall 2027
, Wellesley College will guarantee a tuition-free education to students whose families have a total annual income of $200
,000 or less with typical assets.\n\nThrough this new initiative
, Wellesley College is deepening its commitment to affordability and strengthening its legacy as a leader in providing access
, opportunity
, and educational excellence to students.\n\nAbout Wellesley’s Tuition Promise\nRaising the bar on affordability.\n\nU.S. students whose families have a total income of less than $200
,000 annually
, with typical assets
, will attend tuition-free beginning in fall 2027.\n\nStrengthening our commitment to need-blind admission and financial aid.\n\nWellesley admits U.S. citizens and permanent residents based entirely on their qualifications
, without regard to their financial situations—known as need-blind admission. We meet 100% of every admitted student’s calculated need
, making it financially possible for them to attend and thrive. We are able to do this thanks to the generosity of generations of alumnae who have made gifts to our endowment for this purpose.\n\nOffering sustained financial support throughout a student’s time at Wellesley.\n\nAll eligible incoming first-years
, as well as returning sophomores
, juniors
, and seniors
, will qualify for this expanded level of financial aid starting in fall 2027. This support will continue throughout a student’s time at Wellesley.\n\nFROM THE PRESIDENT\nHOW DOES IT WORK?\nThe Wellesley Tuition Promise\nTOTAL FAMILY INCOME\nUp to $100
,000\n\n100% tuition free\nNo loans\n100% calculated need met\n\nTOTAL FAMILY INCOME\n$100
,000– $200
,000\n\n100% tuition free\n100% calculated need met\n\nTOTAL FAMILY INCOME\nOver $200
,000\n\n100% calculated need met\n\nNote: These examples assume typical assets correspond with income level.\n\n“We are committed to expanding accessibility by making a Wellesley education more affordable. We want to ensure a transformational experience is possible for all talented students who are ready to change the world.”\n\n—T. Peaches Valdes
, Dean of Admission and Financial Aid\n\nFrequently Asked Questions\nWhat does “free tuition” mean at Wellesley?\n\n“Free tuition” is defined at Wellesley as the amount of total grant aid from all sources (institutional
, federal
, state
, and other outside sources) that will meet or exceed the amount of billed tuition for the fall and spring semesters. Financial aid and affording a Wellesley education is a very personalized process. If you have questions
, please contact the Student Financial Services office at sfs@wellesley.edu.\n\nWho qualifies for the Wellesley Tuition Promise?\n\nEligibility is based on the following criteria:\n\nFamilies whose total income is $200
,000 or less with typical assets.\nU.S. citizens and permanent residents.\nStudents and parents/legal guardians who reside and work within the United States.\nStudents who live on campus and participate in the College meal plan.\nAre current students eligible?\n\nYes
, beginning in fall 2027. To be considered for the Wellesley Tuition Promise
, current students will complete their financial aid application annually
, which we will review to determine if they meet the eligibility criteria.\n\nMy family makes more than $200
,000. Are there other financial aid options available?\n\nWe are committed to meeting 100% of the calculated need for all students
, including those whose families make above $200
,000. Financial aid isn’t one-size-fits-all; we evaluate every student’s situation individually. Our review process takes into consideration a variety of factors such as total family income
, number of children in the family
, and the cost of living in the region.\n\nWhat does “calculated need” mean?\n\nCalculated need is the difference between a school’s total cost of attendance and the amount a family is expected to pay.\n\nCost of attendance - family contribution = financial aid offer\n\nHow does Wellesley define total family income?\n\nTotal income includes all sources of income for both parents and students—including both taxed and untaxed income (such as a wage)
, self-employment
, a business
, retirement withdrawals
, payments made to tax-deferred pension plans
, or other benefit distributions.\n\nWhat are “typical assets”?\n\nTypical assets which correlate to the income level at or under $200
,000 may include a modest home equity
, investments
, savings
, and college savings plans among other assets. An amount of a family’s assets are protected from being considered when determining the ability to pay for college. Families with assets significantly higher than what is typical for their income may not qualify for grants meeting the cost of tuition
, but may qualify for need based aid.\n\nWill I still be charged tuition if I meet the criteria for the Wellesley Tuition Promise?\n\nYes. All students will be charged tuition at the start of each semester. Those who complete their financial aid application and meet the criteria will have grants that meet or exceed the cost of tuition. Once awarded
, these grants will be directly applied to the student’s tuition balance.\n\nWill grant funds cover other costs beyond tuition?\n\nIn addition to meeting 100% of one’s calculated financial need
, many students receive grants that exceed tuition to help cover additional direct costs
, such as housing
, meals
, and the student activity fee.\n\nDo I need to reapply for financial aid to be eligible for the Wellesley Tuition Promise?\n\nEligibility for the Wellesley Tuition Promise is reevaluated annually when a student submits their completed financial aid application.\n\nWhat’s next?\nApply to Wellesley\ufeff\nEstimate your cost\ufeff\nSee Cost of Attendance and Payment\ufeff\nContact Student Financial Services\ufeff\nInfo for\nClose'

Since the introduction of test-optional practices at Wellesley, approximately 45% of our enrolling first-year students chose to apply without testing. As always, testing is just one part of our holistic, committee-based application review process. We review each applicant thoughtfully and within the context of their available resources. Regardless of an applicant’s decision to submit test scores, the admission committee is particularly interested in an applicant’s demonstrated writing and quantitative skills as key predictors for success in Wellesley’s curriculum.\n\nThe College will continue to assess the test-optional policy, and we will communicate updates for future application cycles as they become available.\n\nEXPLORE MORE FROM WELLESLEY\nI am first.\n\nThe First Generation Network is a way for first-generation Wellesley students to connect with mentors and each other.\n\nWellesley College admits students of any race, color, national and ethnic origin to all the rights, privileges, programs, and activities generally accorded or made available to students at the school. It does not discriminate on the basis of race, color, national and ethnic origin in administration of its educational policies, admissions policies, scholarship and loan programs, and athletic and other school-administered programs.\n\nInfo for\nClose", 'droped': ''}, {'kept': 'Application Advice\nSkip Sub Navigation\nOur best application advice, just for you.\n\nOur team compiled the best practical pieces of advice to move you toward a successful, and possibly even joyful, application. We offer them based on our own experience, and with total confidence that you will, in the end, blaze your own trail of steps.\n\nStart where you are.\n\nBefore you look at colleges, look (generously!) at yourself.\n\nAsk yourself questions. Such as: What courses have I loved? Why did I love them? What motivates me as a student? Is it intellectual engagement? Creative expression? Solving concrete problems? Discovering strange and unexpected things? Challenging convention?\n\nKeep asking! What motivates me as a human being? What makes me want to get out of bed in the morning? What frustrates me? In what ways do I want to improve? Who’s someone I admire?\n\nStart asking other people! Talk to people who know you well: friends and family, teachers and counselors, coaches and mentors. Talk to representatives at college fairs. Talk to current students. Talk to us. Or be counter-cultural and read the Wellesley 100 (100 of the reasons why we might think you might love Wellesley).\n\nGet organized.\n\nA little planning and preparation goes a long way.\n\nUse tools that work for you. Make a spreadsheet! Command your calendar to send you reminders! Bookmark your favorite online resources! Adjust your spam filter to accept email from your selected colleges! And so on.\n\nFollow the deadlines. Look into required standardized testing and consider the optimal time to take those test(s). Take note of application and financial aid deadlines. Open mail from colleges right away and note schedules for visit programs, information sessions, etc.\n\nPlan a road trip. Try to visit some of the colleges you’re considering. Being on campus, talking to students, feeling the vibe (an actual college admissions term)—these are invaluable. Can’t make it to campus? We’ll do everything we can to give you an immersive online experience.\n\nLook closer.\n\nThere are more than 5,000 colleges and universities in the United States. You probably shouldn’t apply to all of them.\n\nMake a list of essentials. For example: Do you want to be close to a city? Close to home? Do you want a huge university, a small college, or something in between? Are you looking for special programs or majors? (See below for a little counterintuitive advice.) Try to generate a list of 10 to 20 schools that meet your essential qualifications. Then be ready to revise it.\n\nQuestion what’s essential. For example (this is the advice we mentioned above): Your major is not your life. Most students change majors at some point in their college career. And a major isn’t an ironclad, lifetime commitment. On a related note, ponder the value of a liberal arts education—an opportunity to explore a range of fields, make connections across disciplines, and practice the skills that any income-earning, change-making person needs.\n\nConsider the excellence of women’s colleges. We’re just the tiniest bit biased here. And yet, it just so happens that one of the world’s most powerful social and professional networks is made up of our alumnae. Who happen to be women. Not really a coincidence, to be honest.\n\nWhen you’re ready to think about cost, be bold.\n\nYes, be bold. Apply to colleges based not on your family income but on your achievements and your aspirations.\n\nLook at the numbers. You’ll want to get this information from every college on your list: What percentage of students receive financial aid? What forms of aid are provided? What’s the amount of the average annual aid package? What’s the average loan debt of the most recent entering class?\n\nGet a rough sense of the true cost. Most selective liberal arts colleges (which may have a higher sticker price) offer financial aid to support families with calculated financial need. The result is that many families pay less (often a great deal less) than the listed price of the college—less even than they would pay at a public or state institution. Every college has a Net Price Calculator on its website—a tool that gives you a rough estimate of what your family would expect to pay. We also have our own MyinTuition calculator, a simple, straightforward tool developed by one of our professors.\n\nPay attention to deadlines! The deadlines for the FAFSA and CSS Profile really matter. A late application can affect your eligibility for financial aid.\n\nWrite an essay you’ll love.\n\nWhich might be different than an essay you think a college will love.\n\nSet reasonable expectations. You don’t need to have had extraordinary experiences to write an extraordinary essay. You don’t need to have done incomparable things to be interesting. An essay about a small, seemingly insignificant event can be more powerful than an essay about how you’ve saved the world or overcome tragedy. Also: You’re not writing a novel. You should be able to do the work in about 500 words.\n\nFollow a reasonable process. Write several drafts. Let a draft sit for a few days, then tackle it again. Share drafts with people you trust. Ask them: Does this sound like me? Is it interesting from the start? You know you’re done when you’ve used only the words you need to use to say only what you wanted to say.\n\nGive yourself a pep talk. Such as: I love who I am, and no college will change that. I love my voice, and I’m using it now. This is actually fun! I’m having fun! Even if I’m awkwardly telling myself so! I can’t be anyone other than myself, and that’s enough.\n\nInfo for\nClose', 'droped': ''}, {'kept': 'First-Year Applicants\nSkip Sub Navigation\nWe’re glad you’re thinking of Wellesley!\n\nWe’ve pulled together the nuts and bolts of applying as a first-year student right here, just for you. Since the introduction of test-optional practices at Wellesley, approximately 45% of our enrolling first-year students chose to apply without testing. As always, testing is just one part of our holistic, committee-based application review process. We review each applicant thoughtfully and within the context of their available resources. If you have any questions after making your way through this page, we recommend hopping over to the FAQs!\n\nDates and deadlines\nApplication checklist and credit guidelines\nCollaboration agreement\nWho can apply?\nTransfer Applicants\n\nThe Office of Admission expects transfer applicants to have completed at least two full semesters of coursework at another accredited college or university. To receive a Wellesley College degree as a transfer student, you must complete at least two years (16 units) of coursework at the College. Therefore, only incoming college sophomores and juniors are eligible to apply. All courses at Wellesley are in person, and students must attend full-time. If you’re 24 or older, or a parent or U.S. veteran, see our Davis Degree Program for nontraditional-aged transfer students.\n\nThe College does not admit transfer students from online colleges or universities. Individuals who have completed a bachelor’s degree elsewhere, domestically or internationally, are not eligible to apply to Wellesley.\n\nDavis Degree Program Applicants\n\nThe Elisabeth Kaiser Davis Degree Program was designed for women beyond the traditional college age. The program is for those who have begun a bachelor’s degree and wish to complete it at Wellesley. This specifically includes U.S. veterans, parents, and any woman aged 24 or older who has not yet completed their bachelor’s degree.\n\nThe Davis Degree Program brings together scholars from many different backgrounds and life experiences, providing them with a fully engaged college experience at one of the best liberal arts programs available and, in the process, creates an extraordinary community. Davis Scholars take the same classes as Wellesley’s traditional-aged students, have the same degree requirements, and graduate with the same Wellesley degree. Davis Scholars have their own dean who will guide them through the curriculum at the College, along with their major advisors. They can even choose to live in on-campus housing, although family housing is unavailable.\n\nMore questions?\n\nVisit our FAQ page or reach out to us over email. Please invite each of your college recommenders to complete your recommendation letters within the Common Application. Recent academic evaluations are more useful to the Office of Admission. If you have been out of school for several years and are therefore unable to obtain academic evaluations, you may submit one from an employer or a supervisor in a community activity where you have worked. Evaluations/recommendations from friends and relatives are discouraged.\n\nMidterm report\n\nThe midterm report must be completed by applicants currently enrolled in college courses. You may self-report midyear grades, but if you are admitted and choose to enroll, you will be required to submit official grades. Please list the courses you are taking in the current semester on this form and return it to our office immediately following the midpoint of the semester. The form gives us a sense of how you’re doing in your current coursework, so ideally you would fill it out closer to midterms. If you turn in a form with no grades, it will not be considered in your file.\n\nStandardized test scores (optional)\n\nWellesley College will continue to be test optional for those applying for entry in fall 2027. Neither the registrar nor the Office of Admission will supply an estimation before that point. Prospective transfers can get a sense of what courses will transfer by visiting our course catalog. The College will consider awarding transfer credit only for courses similar to those taught at Wellesley.\n\nIf you are applying from an international institution, you can request a credit evaluation of your previously completed university courses following admission and arrival on campus in the fall. Some of your earlier academic work may be applied toward the fulfillment of Wellesley’s requirements. Credit will be granted only for those courses determined by the College’s registrar to be comparable to courses in Wellesley’s curriculum. To gain credit for previous coursework, you must first successfully complete one year of study at Wellesley.\n\nCourses that may transfer\n\nThese types of courses generally transfer to Wellesley:\n\nEconomics from a community college.\n\nLab science from a community college, with department approval.\n\nSingle-semester introductory or intermediate language course. Note: These will not qualify for transfer until the second semester of that level is complete.\n\nFreshman seminars (syllabus required for evaluation).\n\nCourses for which credit may not transfer\n\nThese types of courses typically do not transfer to Wellesley:\n\nCourses that are not classified as liberal arts and sciences, and are not offered at Wellesley.\n\nCourses graded pass/fail, unless you submit a letter from the professor who taught the course, stating that you would have earned a grade of C or higher.\n\nCourses for which you earned a grade of C-minus or below.\n\nMathematics subjects prior to calculus (precalculus, college algebra, trigonometry).\n\nBusiness (management, finance)—except for one accounting course.\n\nCommunications, including speech.\n\nComputer science courses that cover microcomputer applications or nonprogramming topics.\n\nCriminal justice (to be evaluated on a case-by-case basis).\n\nJournalism.\n\nPhysical education or dance, lab-only, and nonacademic studio art.\n\nDistance or online learning.\n\nIndividual study or self-paced courses.\n\nInternships, practica, workshops, how-to courses, or experiential learning.\n\nAdditional considerations\n\nKeep this information in mind when thinking about the transfer of credit:\n\nOne unit of Wellesley College work typically equals four semester-hours or six quarter-hours. That means if you attended an institution where you took five courses each semester worth three credits each, each course could transfer as 0.8 or 0.75 units of Wellesley College work, depending on the total number of credit hours needed for the degree.\n\nStudents may transfer in a maximum of 16 units, only four of which may be earned in summer sessions.\n\nDistribution requirements (except for writing) must be fulfilled by taking or transferring in one whole unit of coursework.\n\nEXPLORE MORE FROM WELLESLEY\nEverything About Us, All at Once\n\nThe definitive (more or less) overview.\n\nInfo for\nClose'}, {'kept': 'Transfer and Davis Degree Program Applicants\nSkip Sub Navigation\nWe’re happy you’re thinking of Wellesley!\n\nEach year we expect to enroll a small number of transfer students, including the special cohort of those in the Davis Degree Program, for entrance in September. Since the introduction of test-optional practices at Wellesley, approximately 45% of our enrolling students chose to apply without testing. Regardless of an applicant’s decision to submit test scores, the admission committee is particularly interested in an applicant’s demonstrated writing and quantitative skills as key predictors for success in Wellesley’s curriculum.\n\nThe College will continue to assess the test-optional policy, and we will communicate updates for future application cycles as they become available.\n\nVisual arts, music, and theatre portfolios (optional)\n\nIf you have a special talent in the arts, you might want to submit an Arts Portfolio. Note: These will not qualify for transfer until the second semester of that level is complete.\n\nFreshman seminars (syllabus required for evaluation).\n\nCourses for which credit may not transfer\n\nThese types of courses typically do not transfer to Wellesley:\n\nCourses that are not classified as liberal arts and sciences, and are not offered at Wellesley.\n\nCourses graded pass/fail, unless you submit a letter from the professor who taught the course, stating that you would have earned a grade of C or higher.\n\nCourses for which you earned a grade of C-minus or below.\n\nMathematics subjects prior to calculus (precalculus, college algebra, trigonometry).\n\nBusiness (management, finance)—except for one accounting course.\n\nCommunications, including speech.\n\nComputer science courses that cover microcomputer applications or nonprogramming topics.\n\nCriminal justice (to be evaluated on a case-by-case basis).\n\nJournalism.\n\nPhysical education or dance, lab-only, and nonacademic studio art.\n\nDistance or online learning.\n\nIndividual study or self-paced courses.\n\nInternships, practica, workshops, how-to courses, or experiential learning.\n\nAdditional considerations\n\nKeep this information in mind when thinking about the transfer of credit:\n\nOne unit of Wellesley College work typically equals four semester-hours or six quarter-hours. This means that our admission decision is based solely on your achievements, talents and promise. Your and your family's financial situation does not negatively impact your consideration for admission.\n\nI’m a non-U.S. citizen. Can I be considered for admission to Amherst and financial aid?\n\nYes! Amherst College welcomes first-year and transfer applications from international students, including those under DACA, undocumented individuals, those with fragile citizenship, and non-citizen permanent residents. About 10 percent of students enrolled at Amherst are international students. We provide support services through our International Students Office. Amherst is one of the very few U.S. colleges to offer need-blind financial aid for international students, just as we do for domestic students. “Need-blind” means that, when evaluating your application, Amherst will not take into account whether you have applied for financial aid.\n\nIs financial aid available for transfer students?\n\nYes. We treat transfer applicants just like first-year applicants. You are considered for admission on a need-blind basis and your calculated need is met in full.\n\nFinancial Aid FAQs\n\nLooking for information about financial aid? Keep exploring - it's all here.
